Two protections are wanted: nobody must be able to appropriate the project to turn it into a closed commercial product, nor take it "their own way" under the same name. The AGPL forces anyone who runs the code (even as an online service) to publish their modifications; CC BY-NC-SA forbids commercial use of the world, the lore and the documents, and requires share-alike. The game's name and its identity remain the property of the project lead.
- **Consequences**: the hidden rules (karma, the Emperor's tree, titles) are **content**, in a second **private** repository loaded by the production server; the public repository contains dummy values so that everyone can compile and play. This private repository is created the day the first hidden rule appears. The project lead keeps the final say on what goes into the project (`CONTRIBUTING.md`). Contributions are accepted under the same licenses.

### D31 — Spatial scale: one tile is about one metre, on a single continuous grid
- **Reason**: cities must be able to grow from one hut to walled towns built by players, and the player must enter a house and see its rooms. That needs the tile to be the smallest thing that takes room: a body, a door, a wall segment, a bed, a fire. A hut is then 3×3 tiles, a house with rooms 6×8 to 10×12, a rampart one tile thick and as long as its builders' patience. Interiors stay on the same grid rather than opening a separate map: a house is walls on tiles, rooms are what walls enclose, the roof is hidden when a watched body is inside. This keeps everything physical: fire spreads, sound carries, witnesses see through a door (D18), a stone rampart is as many demolition sites as it has segments, and no system needs a special case for "door". Separate maps cost the same storage and bandwidth (the tiles exist either way); they save rendering complexity and lose coherence. A region (D07) is what a separate map is for: caves, other worlds — not houses.
- **Consequences**: the first valley is 3072×3072 tiles (about thirty-six minutes of walking across; 8 November 2026, it was 256×256, then 768×768 and 1536×1536 the same day). It is meant to grow tenfold, a hundredfold and more: nothing on the client or the server may cost in proportion to the whole map rather than to the view around a body. A walking step takes about 0.7 s. The grid is cut into **chunks of 32×32 tiles**, the unit of storage, of transfer to clients and of interest (D08): a client receives the chunks around its body. Terrain is **generated from parameters** and never stored tile by tile; what changes the world is objects placed on tiles, through the journal (D17). One level only for the first version; floors are an open question (`06`). Rendering draws only the visible chunks; the client needs a camera (zoom, pan). A path search explores at worst all the ground a body can reach, which on 3072×3072 tiles is already slow for a target across a river without a ford; hierarchical paths come with many NPCs (D10, D11) and will be needed sooner as the map grows.
- **Amended on 10 November 2026 — a body takes the ways it sees, otherwise it walks straight** (stage 0 of the world-by-chunk proposal): a path search stays within what the body sees, a square of **288 tiles on each side** (`PATH_REACH`, the view, D08). A ford, a bridge, a way round a pond in sight is taken, exactly as the whole-map search found it; nothing beyond sight is ever searched, so the body never finds by magic a way the player has not seen. When there is no such way, the body is not refused: it **walks straight toward the target and stops before the first tile it may not enter**, and the player finds the way round (the land is knowledge, `03` §11: no map is shown). So no search explores more than 577 × 577 tiles, about 50 to 100 ms at worst in a release build, whatever the size of the world, where a target across a river with no ford on the 3072 valley used to explore its whole bank, 1.9 million tiles. The search's memory follows the chunks it touches, never the map's size. Planned, not built: re-planning with what comes into sight as the body walks, waypoints the player sets, and sight hidden by groves, rocks and hills (with perception, D18); NPCs and animals will get their own way of moving (D10, D11). A first plan, a budget of expanded tiles with legs toward the closest tile reached, was dropped as more complex than a game where each player steers one body needs. (Later the same day the square shrank to what a body sees from its terrain, under a budget: D32.)

### D32 — Walking in eight directions, with weighted steps
- **Reason**: with only four directions every oblique walk is a staircase, visible at one metre per tile and worse once animals flee and hunters chase. The movement model is a foundation for routines, hunting and carrying: changing it later means retesting all of them.
- **Consequences**: paths use A* over eight neighbours; a diagonal step costs √2 straight ones (10 and 14 in path units) and is never allowed to cut a corner between two impassable tiles. Ties break deterministically.
- **Amended on 5 November 2026 — fine positions and taut paths**: eight directions still make every oblique walk a staircase, and finer grids only make finer staircases. So a body stands at a **fine position** (millimetres) inside the one-metre grid, which stays the unit of everything else (items, buildings, occupation, perception, the eight-direction facing of combat). A walk is planned on the grid, **weighed by the content's cost of each terrain** (`content/movement.ron`: a road is cheap, a forest dear, water impossible), then **pulled taut** into straight segments that cross only tiles no dearer than those they replace — so a road is taken when it is worth the detour and never short-cut through the field beside it. The body advances along the segments every tick at the terrain's speed; it crosses tiles and stops exactly on the target's centre. Clients receive the route once (`body_walks`) and interpolate, instead of one message per metre. Paths cost the same as before; hierarchical paths still come with many NPCs. (10 November 2026: a walk with no path in sight goes straight toward its target until something stands in the way, D31. A body that a straight segment leaves a few millimetres into a tile it may not enter, grazing its corner, walks on at the slowest pace instead of standing stuck.) (12 November 2026: the tile a segment starts in is left whatever it costs, so a body under a tree sent onto the grass, or sent elsewhere while walking, sets off straight from where it stands instead of to the centre of a tile beside it first.)
- **Amended on 10 November 2026 — a body searches only what it sees, and only so hard**: the view's 288-tile square (D31) still let one unreachable click cost 62 to 145 ms on the simulation thread, against a tick of 100 ms, and with 100 walking bodies the worst tick reached 449 ms; with NPCs planning walks by the hundred it could never hold. The project lead's decision: **no help beyond the player's waypoints; exploring and knowing the ways is the game**. So a walk is planned within what the body **sees from the terrain of its own tile** (`content/movement.ron`, `sight_tiles`: about 60 m in the open, 12 m under the trees; 25 m since 12 November 2026, see below), under a **budget of expanded tiles** (`path_budget_tiles`, about 1,500: the way round an obstacle thirty or forty tiles across in the open, nothing wider), and the world runs **at most a few dozen searches a tick** (`searches_per_tick`, about 40), the rest waiting for the next tick in the order they came, deterministically. Within sight and budget the path is exactly the one the larger search found; past either, the body walks straight toward its target and stops at the first thing in the way, as before. A refusal then costs under half a millisecond and a tick of forty searches under 20 ms, whatever the ground; the search's memory follows the chunks within sight, a few pages instead of 361. All three numbers are content, tuned by play. Planned, not built: a chunk-level connectivity check before the search (it would need labels that cost in proportion to the map, which D31 forbids, or a new structure per chunk; with the budget it would save under half a millisecond a refusal), re-planning with what comes into sight, waypoints the player sets, perception (D18).
- **Amended on 12 November 2026 — a body sees out of a wood, and looks again when a wall stops it** (the project lead's decisions after play): a 2-tile way round, in sight on screen, was refused because the body stood under the first trees of a wood, which saw 12 m. Three changes, the budget and the cap kept: (1) **the forest sees 25 m** (`sight_tiles`); (2) the sight is the content's for the **most open walkable ground the body stands on or touches** (its tile and the eight around it, `World::sight_tiles_at`): at a wood's edge it sees out as from the open, in the heart of a wood it sees the forest's; (3) a body that found no way and walked straight **looks again when it stops** before the obstacle, from where it stands and with the sight from there, toward the same target (kept on the body, `Goal`, saved in the snapshot only when there is one; old snapshots read unchanged). A way found is taken; none, it walks straight again, and it looks again at each stop only **from nearer the target than where it last looked**, so it never circles before a wall; when it cannot get nearer it stops for good (it then tells its pilot so: next amendment). These looks count in the cap of searches a tick, after the orders, and wait for the next tick beyond it. Still no help past what the body sees: the way round a wall is found only once the wall's end is in sight from where it stopped.
- **Amended on 12 November 2026 — a blocked body says so** (lightness plan F4, the project lead's decision; protocol v20): a body that stops for good short of its target (it walked straight for want of a way, looked again and found none, and can get no nearer) used to stand there silently. The simulation now tells it once, after its stop, as `BodyBlocked { body, at, target, reason }`, and the server sends `body_blocked` to the **pilot alone**, as a refusal: what a body sees of its way is its own. The reason is coarse and only what the body sees right ahead of it, never what it could not see: the first tile it may not enter along its line toward the target, looked for up to 1.5 m ahead (it stops 30 cm short of it) and a little to each side, so a line slipping between two tiles at a corner sees them: `water`, `rock` (the cliff), or `no_way` when nothing it may not enter stands right ahead (the map's edge, a stop no nearer than its last look). Nothing is told on an arrival, nor at a stop it goes on from after looking again; a new order that cannot even set off is still `move_refused`. The client shows a short line above the body and a small cross at its feet for four seconds, fading, cleared when it sets off again; the words for each reason are kept in one place (`BlockedNotice`), and the drawing is one node taking a line and a place (`BlockedView`), so the body's thoughts, planned as a bubble, can take its place with neither the protocol nor the simulation touched.
- **Amended on 12 November 2026 — trunks block the way, and a body steps round them** (lightness plan F2 and F3, the project lead's decisions: "an adult trunk blocks its tile; a reflex sidestep of 2 to 3 m"): bodies walked through the trees as through air. Now an **adult tree's trunk blocks the tile it stands in** (a seedling's or a young tree's will not, when trees grow in stages; every placed tree is adult today). Each chunk's blocked tiles are a **bitmap of 128 bytes** (32 × 32 bits, `ground::Trunks`), made from the chunk's trees when they are placed and held with them in the world's `Ground` (D37), keyed by the chunk's version, so a tree felled or planted later makes it again; derived, never stored. Path searches never step onto a trunk's tile nor cut between two at a corner (`Map::path_around`), a pulled segment never crosses one (`Map::pull_around`), a teleport stands the body beside one, and a body a snapshot holds in one is let out onto the nearest open tile on load. **The straight walk steps round a trunk or a bush-sized obstacle** (`Map::walk_straight`): when the line meets something no bigger than a tile or two, a tiny search (4 tiles around the stop, 81 tiles at most) finds the way from the stop back to the line 0.5 to 3 m beyond it, pulled taut from where the body stands and never turning back (each turn ahead of the one before), and the walk goes on along its line; for obstacles within the body's sight, 32 at most, the walk stopping before the next, from where the body looks again. Nothing larger is helped: a wall, a river, a thicket still stop the straight walk as before. The search's budget and the cap are unchanged; the client already draws the trees, nothing on the wire changes.
- **Amended on 12 November 2026 — a refused order says why, and the body thinks it in a bubble** (the project lead's decision, protocol v21): a body already at the bank, sent across the water, was refused at once with nothing said, where a body that walked there first said why. A new order that cannot even set off now carries the same coarse reason, found the same way along its line toward the target: `MoveRefused { body, target, reason }` in the simulation, `move_refused` with a `reason` on the wire (`no_way` for an unknown body or a development teleport with nowhere to land, which no pilot sees). The client no longer writes the line above the body: the piloted body's thoughts show in a thought bubble at the top right of the screen, one at a time (each at least 2 s when several come, about 5 s then fading), and a thought tied to a place, a stop or a refusal, takes the camera there when clicked. The small red cross at the body's feet stays in the world, so the place is seen too. Every French line of the interface is kept in one place (`client/ui/ui_words.gd`).
- **Amended on 12 November 2026 — the bubbles keep a thread** (the project lead's decision after trying the bubbles): one thought at a time, each gone after a few seconds, lost what the body had thought a moment before. Both bubbles now sit at the top of the screen, speech top left and thoughts top right, and each side keeps its last 20 bubbles as a chat thread: the 3 newest on screen, the newest at the bottom, the two above it smaller and a little transparent. A bubble enters the thread at once, in its order of arrival, instead of waiting its turn (the queue and its minimum display time are gone); the same line told again by the same speaker is kept once, at its new place. Hovering the stack, the wheel moves a focus back to the oldest kept and down again: the bubble in focus is whole and sharp, the ones around it, older above and newer below, smaller, a little transparent and blurred, so a blurred bubble under the focus tells the player he is not on the newest. The blur is a real one and light: the bubbles out of focus are drawn into a small transparent viewport only when what they show changes, and that texture is shown through a nine-tap shader, one quad of the stack's size a frame. A thought tied to a place still takes the camera there when clicked in the history. Left alone 8 s (nothing new, no hover), the stack folds into one small bubble holding the newest line shortened, which the mouse unfolds; left alone 25 s, it goes too and only a small icon stays in its corner (a speech bubble on the left, a round thought on the right), which opens the stack on the newest when clicked. A new bubble opens it at once. The thought bubble is a round oval, no cloud lobes, with the same calm ink line and the three small circles trailing below it.
- **Amended on 12 November 2026 — a cap of work in two lanes, and a body skirts what it cannot see the end of** (the project lead's two decisions after the crowd bench of the same day, which found the CPU wide and the waiting long: a search costs about 7 µs on average, but 70 % of searches aimed out of sight and were refused at once, and under 40 searches a tick the last of a wave of 5,000 orders set off after 12.5 s). Two changes, the sight and the search's budget kept:
  1. **The cap counts work, not searches**: the tiles all searches of a tick may expand (`search_work_per_tick`, 60,000: forty searches at their whole budget, so the worst tick is bounded as before). A search refused at once costs next to nothing (one unit), so a wave of 5,000 orders clears in two ticks. The searches waiting go in **two lanes**, the bodies a player pilots and the others (NPCs, animals); the players' lane has a **guaranteed share** (`player_share_percent`, 50), the others the rest, and a lane's unused share goes to the other. Within a lane, **first come, first served**, orders and **looks again alike**: a stopped body joins its lane on the tick it stops, where it used to take the places left by the orders by body index from 0 each tick, so the last bodies could wait for ever under a flood of orders. The search at the head of a lane runs as long as any of its share is left, even past it, and the next tick repays the excess: none starves. A body has one search waiting at most; a new order takes its place in line. The simulation learns which bodies are piloted from the server before each tick (`World::set_piloted`), an input like the intentions, never stored; the waiting searches are not stored either, as the intentions were not.
  2. **A body skirts an obstacle whose end it does not see** (a real-time heuristic search, after LSS-LRTA*, `content/ai-reserve/review/foule/chemins.md` §5): when nothing in sight leads to the target and the straight walk would stop before an obstacle **in sight** (a target out of sight, or a way round longer than its sight), the body looks within what it sees for the best point **on the edge of its sight**, by walking cost from where it stands plus straight distance to the target (`Map::skirt`), goes there and looks again. Such a point must be **beside the obstacle** (within 2 tiles of ground it may not enter: where the obstacle goes on out of sight) or **past its end** (from it, the line toward the target crosses nothing it may not enter as far as the body sees, now or from its earlier looks), so it follows the shore instead of wandering into the open land behind it. The body **remembers the ground it explored** for that order (`map::LookedOver`: the blocks of 4 × 4 tiles each look expanded, 128 bytes a look, never stored, forgotten on a new order, an arrival or a restart): that ground is searched last and is never again a point to look from, so it does not circle at the bottom of a bay nor walk back past where it has been. It **gives up**, with F4's `BodyBlocked`, when no point on the edge of its sight is new ground, or after `skirt_looks` looks (24) for one order. A look expands at most `skirt_budget_tiles` (15,000, all a body sees in the open, a few milliseconds at worst) within the cap of work above; the lake, bay and river of the tests cost 1,000 to 8,600 tiles a look. Still only what the body sees, no help beyond: it tries the most promising side and does not come back to try the other; the player sends it there. When the point chosen lies on the line toward the target, the straight walk with its sidesteps (#219) is still the move between looks; when nothing at all is in sight to skirt toward the first time, the body walks straight to the obstacle and looks again from there, as before. A target seen on ground it may not enter (a click on the lake) is only walked toward. A new order is refused at once, saying why (protocol v21, amendment above), only when the body can neither set off straight nor find anything to skirt toward: a body at the bank clicked across a river whose banks go on out of sight now walks along the bank instead. `searches_per_tick` is gone from the content.
- **Amended on 12 November 2026 — a cap of work in two lanes, and a body skirts an obstacle once** (the project lead's two decisions after the crowd bench of the same day, which found the CPU wide and the waiting long: a search costs about 7 µs on average, but 70 % of searches aimed out of sight and were refused at once, and under 40 searches a tick the last of a wave of 5,000 orders set off after 12.5 s). Two changes, the sight and the search's budget kept:
  1. **The cap counts work, not searches**: the tiles all searches of a tick may expand (`search_work_per_tick`, 60,000: forty searches at their whole budget, so the worst tick is bounded as before). A search refused at once costs next to nothing (one unit), so a wave of 5,000 orders clears in two ticks. The searches waiting go in **two lanes**, the bodies a player pilots and the others (NPCs, animals); the players' lane has a **guaranteed share** (`player_share_percent`, 50), the others the rest, and a lane's unused share goes to the other. Within a lane, **first come, first served**, orders and **looks again alike**: a stopped body joins its lane on the tick it stops, where it used to take the places left by the orders by body index from 0 each tick, so the last bodies could wait for ever under a flood of orders. The search at the head of a lane runs as long as any of its share is left, even past it, and the next tick repays the excess: none starves. A body has one search waiting at most; a new order takes its place in line. The simulation learns which bodies are piloted from the server before each tick (`World::set_piloted`), an input like the intentions, never stored; the waiting searches are not stored either, as the intentions were not. `searches_per_tick` is gone from the content.
  2. **A body skirts an obstacle once, then looks once more** (the project lead's rule, the same day, after playing a first version that chained looks along the edge of its sight, LSS-LRTA*-like: on the game valley it left the bank for open country, walked 600 m to 1.4 km and stopped in the middle of nowhere, "arbitrary decisions, it never stops"). One decision at a time: when no way to the target is in sight, the straight walk is stopped by an obstacle **in sight** and no way **past it** is in sight either (a path within sight and the search's budget back to its line beyond the obstacle, `Map::past_obstacle`: the end of a wall, a pond seen whole), the body walks straight to the obstacle, then **picks a side** of it (`Map::skirt`): the obstacle is the patch of ground it may not enter right ahead, flooded as far as it spreads within sight, the bank the open ground within 2 tiles of it; along the bank, each way, the point farthest along within five sixths of its sight (50 m in the open, 20 m in a wood); of the two, the one nearer the target as the crow flies (the bank turns toward it, or rounds the obstacle's end), on a tie the side where less of the obstacle is in sight (it ends sooner), then the right. It walks along the bank to that point and **looks once more**: a way seen (to the target, past the obstacle, or the line toward the target open as far as it sees) is taken; otherwise it **stops for good** with F4's `BodyBlocked`. **No second skirt** for the same order, no chain of looks, no wandering: the player decides the next move. The state is a flag on the body (`Skirted`), never stored: a restart forgets it. **The body thinks aloud** at each decision (protocol v22, `body_thinks { body, thought, at }`, to the pilot alone): `skirting { side: left | right }` about the point it walks to ("Pas de passage ici… je longe vers la droite"), `way_seen` after skirting ("Je vois un passage, j'y vais"), `no_way` right after every `body_blocked`, where it stands ("Je ne sais pas où aller"); the client shows them in the thought bubble (`UiWords.THOUGHTS`). On the 3 km valley, five crossings of the river from the camp now end after one skirt of 20 to 50 m with `no_way` (the only fords are 74 to 400 m away, out of sight). A target seen on ground it may not enter (a click on the lake) is only walked toward. A new order is refused at once, saying why (protocol v21, amendment above), only when the body can neither set off straight nor walk along anything (a body on a rock in the river).

### D33 — Combat: slow real time by intentions, exchanges at a tempo, wounds instead of hit points
- **Reason**: this is a game of lives and consequences, not a fighting game. Combat must be deliberate and rare, quick and lethal, heavy with consequences (witnesses, opinions, karma), and the same system for everything that hurts: a predator, a fall, an assassin. It must also be *played* between two players who think and react, and scale to a battle of a hundred soldiers.
- **Consequences**:
  - **Tempo**: an engaged body commits one **gesture** per tempo (about a game second, slowed by fatigue and wounds). Between tempos the player chooses the next gesture; with no choice, the body follows its **guard**. Players are not asked for reflexes but for reading and choice; network latency vanishes behind a second.
  - **Gestures and guards**: strike, feint, parry, dodge, shove, break off — each with a direction and, for strikes, a target limb; three guards (offensive, closed, evasive). They answer each other like a game of reading (a feint beats a parry, a shove beats a parry, a dodge beats a shove), with uncertainty from skill, condition and equipment.
  - **Simultaneous resolution, moves hidden**: the gestures of every engaged body resolve together at the tempo. One sees the opponent's guard and, during the tempo, the **wind-up** of the gesture — late and ambiguous from a skilled fighter, plain from a novice; a feint shows a false wind-up. All of it is computed in the simulation from the content (D15): the weapon's reach, mass, edge and balance from its parts; the protection worn by limb; the body's skill with that family of weapon, learnt by practice; its condition (fatigue, wounds by limb); its facing (eight directions, D32), the measure (reach strikes first as distance closes), the ground; and the number of attackers, each beyond the first taking from the defence.
  - **No hit points**: a hit gives a wound to a limb (light, grave, mortal) that bleeds, hampers, heals over days and scars. Vitality (D34) is what hunger, cold, blood loss and fatigue drain. Death comes from a mortal wound or from vitality at zero. A dead body is a corpse: an item on the ground, like an animal's.
  - **Assassination is combat against someone who does not know**: asleep, from behind, in the dark, or trusting — no parry, a first blow almost always mortal. Perception (D18) and awareness decide it.
  - **War is the same system**: a hundred engaged bodies are a hundred resolutions per game second. Lines, flanking and breaches emerge from positions and facing; orders are group intentions (D19); morale (Willpower, fear, seeing neighbours fall) makes bodies break and run; fatigue wears a line down. There is no battle mode and no arena instance: an arena is a place.
  - **The engagement window** on the client is a *view* of the same world, not an instance: the camera zooms on the engaged bodies and a panel shows both silhouettes — the opponent's is the attack command (click where you strike), one's own the defence command — while the map stays visible around it, so one sees who comes from behind and can break off and flee across it. Commands are **composable by the player** from the full grammar the server accepts (a default panel of a few, more granular ones to add, keys to assign), but **never conditional**: one intention per tempo, no "if he strikes left, parry left" — that is an NPC brain, and a player who has one is a bot. NPCs use exactly the same grammar.
  - Built with the animals (milestone 2), tried against the predator and in the hunt; witnesses, morale and orders with milestone 4. Tempo length, the gesture set and every number are content.

### D34 — The state of a body: vitality, needs, wounds by limb
- **Reason**: needs, combat, animals, illness and aging all act on the same body and must agree on what hurts and what kills, before any of them is built.
- **Consequences**: a body has a **vitality** (0–100), three **needs** (hunger, cold, fatigue, 0–100), a flag for sleep, and **wounds by limb** (head, torso, arms, legs: none, light, grave, mortal; bleeding or not). Needs rise with time and fall with what relieves them (food, warmth, sleep); at their maximum they drain vitality; a full fatigue forces sleep. Vitality regains slowly when nothing drains it. Death comes at zero vitality or from a mortal wound; the body becomes a corpse item and its pilot is told. Rates and thresholds are content (`content/needs.ron`). Wounds (D33) reduce what a limb can do; until combat exists they are carried empty. Clients see only their own body's gauges.
- **Death and reattachment, provisionally**: until milestone 5 brings the choice of body and rebirth, a pilot whose body dies takes the first free body, or watches if none is free.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — thirst, a fat reserve, cold by season and hour** (the vivarium plan, D41; the project lead's decision of 13 November that these rules hold for the game too and are shown in the client). A fourth need, **thirst**: running water is drinkable (river, stream, ford), still water comes later, and carrying water needs a container, so thirst sets where people camp. Hunger is no longer a cliff: the body draws on a **fat reserve** for some game days, grows thin, weak, then dies; there is a ceiling on what a body eats in a day, and fertility depends on energy. **Cold** comes from the air's temperature by season and by hour, against the body's **insulation** (clothes, shelter) and **heat sources** (a fire). Needs vary with age, sex, pregnancy and nursing; the values are content. Reason: without a reserve every lean day kills, and without thirst and seasonal cold the land does not tell people where to live.
  - **Built on 13 November 2026 — thirst and drinking** (lot N, second piece). A fourth gauge, **thirst** (0–100), rises `thirst_per_day` (100: full in a game day without drinking; faster in heat and at work comes later); at full it drains `dehydration_per_day` of vitality (50: two days at full thirst kill, so a body dies on the third day without water), and a body that dies so died `dehydrated`. Placeholder values, in `content/needs.ron` with their reasons. **Drinking** is an intention, `Drink` at a tile on the body's tile or next to it holding **running water** (river, ford, brook; `Terrain::is_drinkable`): the thirst is **gone at once**, the body woken, and its pilot told its condition at once. Why at once rather than over a while: no act lasts in time yet (a meal is eaten at once too), and a body at the water drinks its fill; acts that last come with their own model. No container is needed at the water; carrying water needs one, later. A drink out of reach or away from running water is refused with the reason (`DrinkRefused`: `out_of_reach`, `not_water`). Protocol **v24**: the condition carries `thirst`; `drink` (x, y) and `drink_refused`; the death cause `dehydrated`. The client shows a thirst gauge beside the others and offers "drink" in the context menu on a tile of running water within reach. Snapshot format **v12**: the bodies of a world saved before have just drunk (thirst 0).
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — no idle bodies: a player's body is born at the camp** (the project lead's call of the same day: "we keep only the vivarium; we'll bring them back later"). The three bodies a new world started with by the camp, standing there unpiloted (one greeted the player in `--dev`, a placeholder now removed with its words), are gone, the game staying playable: **a new world holds no body** (`World::with_valley`). **A connection** takes a free body if one is left (its pilot gone, or from a world saved before this change), the lowest id first; **else a body is born for it at the camp**: on the grass nearest `Map::camp()` where no body stands and no trunk grows (`World::spawn_at_camp`). **A pilot whose body died** does the same: a free body if one is left, else a new one born at the camp; it no longer watches for want of a body. The birth is the simulation thread's, before a tick, as it takes the intentions (D24, D29): the connection asks (`Shared::body_for`), the simulation thread bears every body asked for (`Shared::bear_bodies`), registers its pilot, and those who see the camp are told it stands there (`body_stopped`, as for a body walking into their range). The handshake waits for it (a tick, normally; at most the handshake's five seconds, after which the client is welcomed watching and given the body when it comes, `body_assigned`). Reusing free bodies first keeps the world from filling with the bodies of players who left: there are never more bodies than players at once, plus those of an older world. **Why**: the lead keeps only the vivarium for now; bodies nobody pilots come back later. No change of protocol or of the snapshot's format: an older world keeps its unpiloted bodies where they stand, and the next connections pilot them first.

### D35 — Objects and carrying: one place at a time, a hand and a load in kilograms
- **Reason**: picking up, carrying, laying down, eating, crafting, fighting and trading all move objects between bodies and the ground; they must agree on where an object is and what a body can hold, before any of them is built. Tools and weapons are *held*: what is in a body's hand is seen by all.
- **Consequences**: an item is in **exactly one place** at a time — on a tile, carried by a body, or (later) in a container or a structure; never in two, never nowhere. A body carries in two ways: **in hand**, one item, visible to all and the tool or weapon of combat (D33); and **on it**, the rest, a shapeless bag for now. Capacity is in **kilograms** from the materials' masses (`content/movement.ron`: 20 kg to start), not in slots; the walk slows with the load. Acts are intentions within a tile's reach: pick up (into the empty hand, else carried), hold, stow, drop on one's tile, eat from the ground or from what is carried; every act wakes. The **server judges, the client proposes**: the client composes its menu from what it knows, the server refuses what cannot be done and says why. A dead body drops everything. Containers, loads carried by two, objects laid on a table or a stall fit the one-place rule without change.
- **Interface, first form** (decision 6): names on hover; a context menu on right-click (without dragging) on a tile within reach; an inventory panel listing the hand first, with masses and the load. Keys remain as shortcuts.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — carrying without a container** (the vivarium plan, D41; the project lead's decision of 13 November that this holds for the game too and is shown in the client). It replaces "20 kg of anything". The **two hands** hold two objects, or **one heavy object** (a stone, a fox, a log) up to a mass set by the body's **strength**; strength differs from body to body. **Small loose things** (berries, nuts, pebbles) make **a handful** bare-handed; beyond, a container is needed. **Heavy loads tire**, the more the longer they are carried: twenty kilograms for ten minutes, yes; for hours, no. A baby carried takes a hand; long wood is carried as an armful. At the Stone Age start, **a hide tied as a bundle counts as a container**: heavy, one per person, and it holds no water. Reason: the container is one of the first things the vivarium's people must find; a body carrying 20 kg of anything gives them no reason to.
- **Amended on 13 November 2026 — the item model, where an item can be, structures and ownership** (the vivarium plan's checks). An item is a **bulk mass in whole grams** of one material, which **splits and merges** (merging two heaps of different ages follows a content rule), with a **creation date** and a **state** that leads to spoilage (D16). It is still in exactly one place: **in a hand, as a loose handful, carried on the body, in a container, on a structure, or on a tile**. **Structures** (a fire, a rock shelter, a store) stand on tiles and are world state; a shelter has a capacity and does not house everyone. **Ownership is minimal**: what a body carries is its own, the camp's store is the band's; nothing locks it, others recognize it or not (D19). Reason: gathering, eating, spoilage and stores all move matter by weight between bodies and places; whole grams keep it exact and deterministic.
  - **Built on 13 November 2026 — the item model, first step of lot N** (the vivarium plan, "Manger et boire"). An item (`sim::Item`) carries its own **mass in whole grams** (`mass_g`, never 0) and the **tick it was made** (`made`, its date); the state that leads to spoilage comes with spoilage. A material's `mass_kg` is now only the **piece** the ground drops or a death leaves: a natural item (D15, made per chunk) weighs its spawn rule's piece (`Spawn::piece_g`, filled from the material when the content is read) and is dated tick 0, as old as the world; a corpse weighs the corpse material's piece. A pile **splits** (`Item::split`: a given number of grams into a new item, same place and date; never 0 grams nor the whole pile) and **merges** (`Item::merge`: same material only, the masses summed, the **older date wins**). Why the older date and not a mass-weighted one: a heap is as old as the oldest matter in it, so fresh berries poured on old ones do not make the old ones fresh, a store cannot be kept young by topping it up, and the date stays an exact tick, never rounded; keeping heaps apart is the players' choice. Food gives **energy per kilogram** (`food: (energy_kj_per_kg, keeps_days)`, the one scale of D15): berries 2,500 kJ/kg and meat 1,200 kJ/kg give a piece exactly the kilojoules it gave before (250 and 1,200). **A meal has a size**: eating takes at most `meal_g` grams (`content/needs.ron`, 300 g, placeholder), the rest of the item staying where it is (on the ground it is told as gone and laid again, lighter, the chunk's version moving twice); `BodyAte` says what and how much (`material`, `grams`, `energy_kj`). The load is the sum of the items' own grams; the carrying rules are unchanged (20 kg of anything, until the amendment above is built). Protocol **v23**: every item says its grams (`mass_g` on `ItemView` and `ItemState`, and on a chunk frame's stored items, now 16 bytes each), a natural item weighing its material's piece; the client shows an item's own grams in the inventory and on hover. Snapshot format **v11**: an item saved before weighs its material's piece and is dated tick 0. Merging is not yet an act a pilot asks for: gathering and stores will use it.


### D36 — Water as a volume: a river carries a flow that sets its width and depth
- **Reason**: a tile is about a metre (D31), so a tile of water half a metre deep is a half-cube, and a river is a number of half-cubes passing: its **flow**. One number then explains everything the banks need: two waters that meet become one, wider or deeper, never two rivers crossing; a river that parts around an island shares its flow between the arms; a ford is where a river spreads until it is half a metre deep right across, which only a small flow can do; a brook is the same water with a tiny flow. Width, depth, fords, islands, brooks and junctions stop being separate rules with separate dials, and later systems (the current, wading, drowning, boats, fishing, the sea at the end of the network) will read the same depth. It costs almost nothing: the flow is computed once at generation and the depth stored once per tile; nothing flows at every tick.
- **Consequences**:
  - The map keeps a **depth per tile in half-metres** (0 on land, 1 on a ford, up to 12 in a river: 6 m, a great river), regenerated with the terrain, not stored. **Amended on 12 November 2026: the map keeps it for the water tiles only, sparse** (per chunk holding water, each water tile's terrain, depth and current, about 6 bytes; a bit per chunk that it holds any), and drops the rivers' working arrays once the waters are painted: water is about 1.5 % of the land, and a map holding every tile grew with the whole map (57 MB on 3072 × 3072, about 510 MB on a map three times wider), which no larger world could afford. The land around is made per chunk (D37). **Amended on 19 November 2026: on a window of the atlas (generators 3 and 4, the vivarium's world) nothing is scanned at birth**: a chunk's water is made from the window when the chunk is first read (the stretches filed under it and the lakes beside its cell), and the chunks read lately are held (`Waters`, as many as the tiles' cache). The scan of the whole window was 3 s of the vivarium server's start (11.1 → 8.0 s, medians of three alternated starts) and, at 1.5 % water, the last structure under a window that grew with its area; the tiles are the same to the byte (a test over four seeds, and the whole vivarium map hashed before and after). The window's cut reads the region's rivers through an index of where they lie, no longer every stretch of the region for every brook: a window of 8 km in a 65 km region is cut in 0.12 s instead of 34 s, 2 km in 0.04 s instead of 0.96 s, the same window to the byte. Half a metre is crossed easily; a metre can be waded, slowly and without a load, when movement in water is built (a content cost per depth, not code); deeper, one swims or drowns.
  - The valley's waters form a **tree**: a main river from the cliff's foot to the south edge; **tributaries** from the edges and **brooks**, both counted by the map's side rather than its ground (8 November 2026): tributaries enter along the edges, and brooks are long and cross much of a map, so a larger map has them farther apart, not crowded. **22 to 34 tributaries and up to 41 to 65 brooks on 3072×3072** (about 35 to 50 find a place; 10 to 16 brooks on 768×768, where counted by the ground they had been 27 to 54, short and crowded). About two thirds of the brooks **enter by the map's edges** (west, east, south) or rise at the cliff's foot, and head into the valley (at least 30 tiles from that edge), never along an edge: past its first 8 steps a brook keeps 5 tiles from the map's edges and the cliff (along an edge, half of it lay past the ground drawn); the others rise where the ground is wet (a noise of wetness, like the groves; its average is a dial of the generator now and becomes a datum of each region once regions exist, D07) and at least 50 tiles from any water. Each runs to the nearest water, river or brook, **60 to 250 steps long**, never leaves the valley floor (out of the map or into the cliff and back would leave a piece alone), and keeps **25 tiles from the other brooks** except over the last stretch where it comes to join one. A brook **meanders** more than a river (7 November 2026): it holds a heading 2 to 5 steps (a river 4 to 10) and may stray up to 90 degrees from the heading it rose with (a river 67.5), still one step of 22.5 degrees at a time and never turning back. A river born of brooks that join keeps their winding course. Waters are **few** (7 November 2026): they are landmarks, not the landscape; three tributaries and eight to fourteen brooks crowded the valley, and the valley's water lies on **about 1.5 % of its ground** on average (9 November 2026; it was 4 to 5 %, then 3 %, then 2 %, as the project lead asked): fewer tributaries (7 to 12 on 3072 × 3072) and rivers **wider for the same flow** (a wide stretch about √(3.5 × flow) across, it was √(2 × flow), yet never under a metre deep outside a ford: too little water for that is a brook), so the land is cut less and walked more, most of it the rivers; one map alone ranges from 1.5 % to 2.5 %. Every water ends where it meets another; later, the main river reaches a sea.
  - The **section follows the flow**: `width × depth` covers the flow, a stretch being either *wide* (width about √(2 × flow)) or *deep* (about √flow), the choice drawn once per stretch between junctions. Water **one or two tiles wide is a brook** (`Terrain::Brook`, a metre deep at most, walked through); from three wide it is a river, up to 6 m deep; past that it only widens, **with no limit but its flow** (8 November 2026; it was 12 tiles and 4 m, and a river past 96 half-cubes simply lost the rest: on the 3072 valley the main river carries 125 to 224 at its mouth, now 17 to 23 tiles wide and 5 to 6 m deep). A limit of 64 tiles is only a safeguard. Realism first: a valley as wet as this one ends in a great river; drier regions, with fewer tributaries, will have thinner ones.
  - A **ford** is a lens where a river spreads to half a metre right across, so it is always wider than the river around it; only a flow of 12 half-cubes or less can do it, upstream of the junctions that make a river too strong. A river that could have a ford has one **by chance** (a dial, one in two to start), never by right: a ford is a place the terrain gives, rare enough to matter, and a bank without one is a reason for a bridge or a boat. Nothing is forced; a part of the map may wait for them. Only the starting ground is checked to be large enough.
  - A **divergence** splits a river in two arms around an island, from a rock to a large island; the smaller arm, when shallow enough, shoals into a ford, which is how one reaches the island.
  - **The banks breathe** (7 November 2026): a river with both banks parallel for tens of tiles looked drawn with a ruler, and a real bank is rarely straight for long: the two banks drift apart or together, or one bends alone and the river widens there. So each bank of a river, on its own, holds its line for 4 to 12 steps, then turns a quarter of a step of heading (about 5.6 degrees) out or in for 12 to 40 steps and comes back, so a river widens and narrows slowly (a whole step, 22.5 degrees, then half a step, made the river look misshapen: the lead's verdicts the same day); it stays between about a tile inside and six tiles outside its line (three until the lead asked for wider pools, the same day). Both out is a pool, both in a narrows, one out and one in the river sliding sideways, one alone a bend on one side. The width stays from 3 (never a brook) up to 18 for a **pool**, or half again the river's own width for a great river, and the **depth takes what the width leaves**, so the flow is kept: a narrows is deeper, a pool shallower, but **never less than a metre**. A pool wider than its flow can fill a metre deep is slower water holding more than its flow, as in a real basin, rather than a shoal: it never becomes a ford by accident (a ford stays a place chosen as such). The banks hold their lines near junctions, fords, divergences, the map's edges and on brooks, and a bank never bends within a step of a bend of the centre path, so a bank never turns by more than one step at once. The centre path also bends more often: a heading is held 4 to 10 steps (was 6 to 14).
  - **The banks of the window's rivers walk as the valley's do, a cell at a time** (15 November 2026; the project lead's verdict in the game on the vivarium's river of PR #237: "the course is better, but the banks still read as parallel"; then on PR #238, which made them breathe by waves and laid pools, narrows and a grain over them: the pools read as "bubbles", one fixed shape posed every 225 m, and the grain as "cheese nibbled by mice", repeating teeth from a three-tile lattice, while the valley's river, whose banks are walked section by section, was more natural: its widenings arrive anywhere, last, and blend). A continent river drawn by the window (D41, `plan::course`) keeps its course and the ford's lens as they were, and its banks take **the valley's rule** (`map::Rivers::breathe`), **in widths rather than tiles**: on each atlas cell's course, each bank on its own holds its line for 4 to 12 steps, then leaves it at a slope of 5.6° for 12 to 40 steps, out or in, half the moves once off the line going back towards it, at most a fifth of the width in and 1.2 widths out, the whole width between three fifths and three times the one its flow gives; a step is a fifth of the river's nominal width (the valley's tile for its river of five), so a 5 m brook, a 30 m river and a 200 m river look alike at their scale. Both banks hold their lines over six steps at both ends of a cell's course (a cell is walked without its neighbours, D39), around a ford's bar and around a junction. No pools or narrows are posed and the water line has no grain any more. **A tributary joins at first contact**: where its course first comes within one width of the stem's water it joins there, the rest of its course is dropped, and the stem takes its downstream width from the junction on (at the vivarium's east confluence the tributary ran alongside the stem for 300 m before the atlas's node). Brooks and lakes keep their lines. The depth still takes what the width leaves, never under a metre on a river, so a widening never becomes a ford by accident. All of it is drawn from a stream keyed by the cell, so two windows that overlap draw the same banks (D39).
  - **The course is thinned to the river's scale and meandered piece by piece, and the brooks meander too** (15 November 2026, the safety net's third correction; the project lead's verdict on the first sampling: straight reaches in 26 windows of 108, the vivarium's river straight for 675 m, the great rivers of the marsh sheet crossing their 2 km tiles as kinked canals with stepping banks, and the brooks in 45° diagonals and right angles). The region's course (midpoint displacement at the 64 m cell, drawn uniformly and truncated to whole cells) left a straight reach every few cells and, on a wide river, followed the grid's zigzag; the fine detail below the cell could not bend a reach. So each cell's course is first **thinned** to pieces of five widths (two region cells at least, no piece under three quarters of that nor over one and a half), the region's 64 m points between dropped and any corner over 70° dropped as a hairpin; then **meandered**: every piece is bent to one side by 200 to 250 per mille of its length (a bend of 44° to 53°), the pieces to alternate sides, except next to a corner over 30°, which a piece bends away from, and a bend into a corner is capped so that corner and bend together never pass 70°; then **rounded** by corner cutting, each cell over its own run with as many passes as bring its pieces under 4 m (five on a small river, eight on a great one), keeping what lies between the run's own nodes so that two cells rounded differently meet on one curve. With legs of 1.9 widths at least and no vertex over 70°, the rounding's radius stays over the river's width, so the bank on the inside of a bend turns by 20° a step at most and never folds (the fine detail of the first draft, halvings down to a width, shortened the legs and stacked their turns: a 114 m river bent with a radius of 75 m and its inner bank folded; it was dropped). A tributary's course ends on its stem's course as drawn, at the point nearest the atlas's node, paced a cell at a time where the stem cut a hairpin, so the two meet; the stems are drawn before their tributaries. The banks keep the valley's walk; each node reads its banks from the steps around it by a cubic that never overshoots (flat where a move begins or ends), so a move's start and end are no corner on a wide river. **The brooks** no longer keep their lines: a brook's line from its cell's point to its receiver's is meandered by midpoint displacement (three halvings, 64 m down to 8 m, the first bending the line by 120 to 250 per mille of its length, the next ones by half as much each), and each cell draws the piece from the middle of its own line through its receiver's point to the middle of the receiver's line, rounded by two corner-cutting passes, so the corner at every receiver is a bend; a piece is cut inside an innermost, straight piece of the line, so two cells' pieces meet without a corner; a source draws its head too. A cell's piece reads its receiver and its receiver's receiver (two region cells, the window's margin), the river's course reads two continent cells either side (the span's margin), and every draw is keyed by the cells, so two windows that overlap draw the same waters (D39). The safety net's `StraightReach` fell from 26 windows of 108 to none; the vivarium's crossing keeps its ford and the path between the camps wades it. Every number here is a dial of the generator.
  - The rules of the shore stay: straight runs in sixteen headings, a bend is a piece, a bank never cuts through a tile. The client drew the bank in the ground shader from the distance to the water, a distance-field texture (ShoreField, 9 November 2026: the bank pieces, tiles and brushes tried before were retired that day, code and images; the images are kept outside the repository). **Amended on 11 November 2026: the bank is drawn from vertex data on two meshes per wet chunk, not from a texture** (`WaterMeshes`). The same smoothed line (the tiles' water mask blurred three times, read as a signed distance) is sampled on a lattice of four points a tile and carried, as a float, by the vertices of a *bank mesh* over the band along the line; a *water mesh* covers the water more than a tile from any bank. Each vertex also carries, as bytes, the current, the depth (a proxy from the distance to the line until the server sends its byte), the ford and the sand, and the bank mesh the bank's width after its wander, beaches and pinch, computed once a vertex instead of once a pixel. The reason: the ground shader searched the bank at every pixel of every water quad (the field, its gradient, the width's wander, the beach search, the tufts, the lap: ten to forty texture reads); the meshes' shaders read two to five, and nothing is searched. Measured on the project lead's machine (3072 × 1728, F8: medians of 300 frames with and without the water, alternated), the water's GPU time at a river with banks, a ford and the great river fell by **3 to 8 times with one water layer, 2 to 3 times with the two layers and the current's streaks the project lead chose** (at a river with banks (tile 516, 1844, zoom 1) the water's share of the GPU frame 29–33 % → 14–19 %, at a ford (1468, 1640, zoom 1) 45–48 % → 17–23 %, at the great river (1468, 2643, zoom 0.4) 43–46 % → 25–28 %; in milliseconds, where the GPU clock allowed a comparison (600–700 MHz), 3.2–5.9 → 1.1–1.7 ms, 6.1–6.7 → 1.8–2.2 ms, 3.6–4.0 → about 1.5 ms). Nothing is uploaded per block: two chunks compute the same numbers for the lattice points they share, so the meshes join without a seam. The brooks are a mesh of ribbons of their own (`BrookLines.mesh`), each vertex carrying **its own tile's current** (never an average over the tiles around, which mixed the two legs of a meander and ran stretches upstream) and, at a mouth, the fade into the river's water read from the river's line at the vertex. The lead's look: two water layers (`flow_mix` 0.45), no warp, the streaks where the water runs faster than 0.3 m/s, full at 1 m/s ("c'est vraiment super, c'est vraiment trop joli"). The server's depth byte comes with a protocol change, later, under the lead's label.
  - **The water runs** (8 November 2026): every tile of water knows where it runs, the heading of the course that painted it, and how fast, from its section: `BASE_SPEED` (35 cm/s) times the flow over what the section holds, so a pool is slow, a narrows quick, and a ford, spread thin over its stones, half as fast again. Kept per tile of water with the map (sparse since 12 November 2026, above), regenerated, not stored; sent with each chunk (`currents`, protocol v11) so the client can make the water flow the right way at the right pace. The client cannot tell upstream from downstream from the tiles alone.
  - The client receives `Water`, `Ford` and `Brook` (protocol v9); depth reaches it when something is drawn from it. Every number here is a dial of the generator, to be tuned by looking at maps; the mechanism is the decision.

### D37 — Woods as a density: massifs, edges, clearings, copses, and species in stands
- **Reason**: the forest was one noise cut at one level: patches of one size scattered everywhere, a tile all forest or all grass, blind to rivers and cliff. A real valley has one or two great woods and open meadows, a wood thins out at its edge, opens in clearings, sends copses and lone trees into the meadow and lines its rivers. One number per tile, the **density of trees** (0 to 100), says all of it, the way the flow says the water (D36); and it is what will place the trees when they become things standing on the ground: many and close in the heart, a few at the edge, one alone in the meadow. Later it can also set how slowly one walks and how far one sees under the trees (a rule of play, not of shape: not yet).
- **Consequences** (decided 8 November 2026):
  - The map keeps per tile a **density of trees** and a **habitat** (meadow, edge, wood, heart, clearing, camp, copse, lone tree, riverside), regenerated with the terrain, not stored. **Amended on 12 November 2026: made per chunk when read, never for the whole map.** A chunk's terrain comes from the woods drafted on the chunk alone (every rule above depends only on the seed and what lies within a bounded distance: the waters' pull within 61 tiles, round patches decided cell by cell), its density and habitat also from the forest's depth, which looks 55 tiles away (the chunks two around it); both are held in bounded caches, the least recently used out first (about 8 MB and 3.5 MB when full), each thread keeping its last four chunks of each at hand (a tile read takes no lock), and the round patches decided lately in a memo. The far view, 2 km of land counted tile by tile, is counted on the chunk pool after the chunks of the view, held by its centre and sent when ready, never on the network's threads. The reason: the map's memory followed its whole area (the woods' arrays, 2 bytes a tile, and the terrain's); it now follows the water and the chunks in use. The world is the same tile for tile (the fingerprint tests are unchanged); a chunk read the first time costs its making (a fraction of a millisecond), where the whole map was made at startup. A tile is `Forest` from a density of 35 up: the wood and the heart. The edge, copses' rims, lone and riverside trees stand on grass. The client still receives the terrain only; the protocol does not change.
  - **Massifs**: a broad noise (64 tiles) roughened by two finer ones (16 and 5 tiles), thickened where the ground is wet (the wetness the brooks rise from) and in a band ten rows deep at the cliff's foot. The level that makes a tile wooded is **chosen per map**, so the massifs, edges included, always cover **32 %** of the land: with one or two massifs a map, a fixed level gave anything from a third to three quarters.
  - **Edge, wood, heart**: the density rises from a massif's rim inwards over a few tiles; the heart (from 75) never touches the open meadow.
  - **Clearings**: ten a map in the woods, 3 to 7 tiles in radius, ragged; and a **camp clearing** 9 tiles around the place the first bodies start (`Map::camp`, tile 134, 139 of the first valley).
  - **Copses**: thirty a map in the meadow, 1.5 to 4 tiles in radius; **lone trees** on about 0.4 % of the meadow; **riverside trees** on the bank, two tiles from a river with a chance of 25 %, three tiles 15 % (8 November 2026: at one tile with 35 % they stood half in the water and were too many).
  - Over six maps the land is a third wooded (33 to 35 %), a fifth or so forest proper.
  - **Several species, grouped in stands that mix** (the lead's call): each habitat has its species (heart and wood: oak, pine, some birch; the band at the cliff's foot, its own habitat: pine; edge and clearings' rims: birch, hazel; riverside: willow; copses and lone trees: oak, wild apple, from `09-visual-production.md`). Each species also has its own **stands**, a noise about twenty tiles across (worked out at the point, nothing held for the map: 10 November 2026); a tree's species is drawn from the habitat's weights times the square of each species' stand there. Trees of one kind come in groups that mix at their borders: on the test maps about one neighbour in five was of another kind with the first trees, 425 in a thousand once trees kept their room (D40), and 621 in a thousand on the grid (10 November 2026), where a tree's neighbours stand a cell away. The weights are content (`content/species.ron`), not code; balanced weights matter as much as the stands, since a species that outweighs the others everywhere leaves no room for groups of the others.
  - **The trees themselves** (8 November 2026; placed on a grid on 10 November 2026, the project lead's choice on images): the woods' trees stand on a **jittered grid** (the scheme of D24's amendment) of 4 m cells, every other row shifted by half a cell so that no rows show along the view's diagonals; a cell holds one tree at most, up to 1.4 m off its centre (so trees keep 1.2 m apart by construction, with no check between them), with a chance of 11 thousandths per point of the density of the tile it lands on, never more than 90 %, and a nested cell of 3 × 3 cells (12 m) is left empty one time in twenty: even a heart has gaps and small openings (a heart holds a tree on about one tile in twenty). A lone or riverside tile holds its own tree, up to 30 cm from its centre; nothing stands on a tile touching a river or a ford, even by a corner, since its trunk and crown would be drawn over the water. They are placed **chunk by chunk** when a chunk is asked for (`trees::trees_in`, about 15 µs a chunk; the 3072 valley's 83,000 trees in 0.12 s where planting them all at load took 0.42 s), from the map and the content, never stored and no longer held for the whole map; a felled tree will be stored when felling comes and laid over them. A tree does not block the way for now: one walks under its crown, the forest's own cost slowing the walk. Chunks carry their trees and `world_state` the kinds of tree with their height (protocol v10). The client draws each tree as an image of its kind standing on its foot, sorted with the bodies from far to near so a body walks behind a trunk; which image of the kind and a size within 12 % are drawn from the tree's place, as with the bank pieces, with a hash the client shares with the server's generator, bit for bit (`StableHash`, 10 November 2026: Godot's own `hash()` may change with the engine), and never from the seed.
  - **The flora** (9 November 2026, the project lead's list `content/ai-reserve/notes/10-flora.md`): shrubs, plants and mushrooms in `content/flora.ron`, with the four trees still to come. Each kind has a **niche**: the places it grows (the habitats above, plus `bank` within two tiles of water, `stream` in a brook, `shallows` in a ford), the soil's moisture (from the distance to water and the valley's wetness) and the light (from the density of trees) it needs, and for some mushrooms a host tree, near it or on its trunk. Patches start where the niche holds (a single plant, a clump, a carpet, a fairy ring), with a chance set by its density times its **stand** (a smooth noise per kind: kinds come in places and mix where their stands meet) and the ground's **breath** (thick here, sparse a little further); each plant of a patch stands where its kind grows, and plants with volume keep their room. Placed **chunk by chunk when a chunk is asked for**, from the seed, nothing stored (D39); the view around the camp before the first connection. The server keeps the plants of the chunks asked for most recently only (a cache of `PLANT_CACHE_CHUNKS` chunks, several views' worth: 9 November 2026), so its memory no longer grows with the ground visited; a chunk forgotten is placed again, the same. A change to a plant (picked, trampled) must therefore be stored apart and applied on top, never only in that cache. What a plant does to whoever eats or touches it is hidden content (D30) and never here. One season for now: everything grows all year.
  - **Space** (9 November 2026, with the trees at their true height, D40): the massifs cover **24 %** of the land (it was 32 %) on a noise twice as broad (128 tiles), so the woods are fewer and larger and the plains wide; copses fewer but bigger (10 for every 256 × 256 tiles, 4 to 11 tiles in radius) and lone trees (0.09 % of the meadow) dot the plains; trees stand wider apart where the wood is thin (their room grew from the heart's to 1.6 times it; on the grid, 10 November 2026, a thin wood leaves more cells empty), so an open wood shows gaps and a heart closes; riverside trees are about half as frequent, so the river and its banks show.
  - **A natural forest, 10 November 2026** (the project lead's plan, approved on the map seen from above): the trees grow as a real forest does, in layers, still chunk by chunk from the seed (`sim::trees`, nothing stored). **Stands**: one kind rules a stretch of a few hundred metres, the kind whose smooth noise (192 tiles, roughened by 48) is strongest among those the habitat suits at least half as well as the best; two stands blend over a few dozen metres. **Mother trees**, one every 24 m: a tree takes the kind of a mother near it, the nearer the likelier, as far as that kind's seeds fall (`spread_m` in `species.ron`: an acorn 12 m, a birch seed 34 m), weighed by how its own habitat suits that kind; so companions come in groves mixed with the ruling kind at their rim, not in pure patches. **Openings**: a 16 m square is now and then open, where only a few pioneers (birch) grow back. **Edges** thin with the density and lean to edge kinds (birch, hazel, ash). **Near the cliff** (within 18 to 54 tiles of the rock, varying tile by tile) the woods grow as at its foot: pines. **Riverside** trees stay one a tile on the bank, alders and willows in runs from their mothers. The reason: a forest of one draw per tree was salt and pepper; real woods are mosaics of stands, groups around seed trees, edges and openings.
  - **A wild valley, 10 November 2026** (the project lead, after trying the natural forest: "far too sparse, the land between too empty"; then, after trying a valley seven tenths forest, "a little more open"). The world is untouched by man and full of powerful forests: **forest covers a little over half the land** (55.3 % of the land tiles of the 3 km valley, water and cliff left out; it was 19 %). The woods layer changes with it, and becomes **local**: no level chosen for the whole map (a fixed `LEVEL`), no stream drawn across it; massifs from a hash noise of 320 tiles roughened by 64 and 12; large clearings (12 to 36 tiles in radius), small woods of 50 to 200 m in the open between the massifs, and copses (2.5 to 6 tiles, tight, a few to thirty trees) laid cell by cell on grids in absolute coordinates, each from a hash of its cell (`Woods::patches`); **meadows along the rivers** (the woods' pull falls within 40 tiles of a river); **lines of trees along the brooks**; riverside trees from a hash per tile (the meadows and the brook lines are replaced by the woods along the waters, 11 November 2026, below). The edge keeps its depth (about 5 tiles). **The trees thicken from the edge to the heart**: the forest's density counts how deep a tile lies in the forest (a distance capped at 55 tiles, in two sweeps): the forest's own (35) over the first 18 tiles, open crowns and the ground seen between them, then rising steadily to full at 55 tiles; small woods and copses keep their quicker rise. The ground (where the forest is) is unchanged by it. In the meadows, trees come in **small groups of 3 to 8**, by the woods or the water and rarer farther; a tree alone far from both stands about once a square kilometre (the lone-tree habitat is no longer drawn). **Men will fell these forests, and they will not grow back by themselves** (seeds and replanting, maybe later): whole primeval forests can be cleared by players over years, by their own choice; the world starts as wild as it will ever be. Nothing of felling is built yet. Measured on the 3 km valley (best of three alternated runs, release): 173 531 trees (83 498 before the change), the heart 4.8 trees a 100 m² (5.3 before), the woods around it 2.4 (3.5); `trees_in` about 71 µs a chunk (11 before); a chunk's whole ground as the server sends it (its natural items, 31 now against 11, and its plants among the trees of nine chunks) 2.75 ms (2.2 before), most of it the plants as before; the valley made in 2.0 s (1.1). The valley's terrain, items and trees fingerprints are re-pinned on purpose.
  - **The trees of a chunk placed once, 11 November 2026** (lightness plan, D1). A chunk's trees were placed again every time they were read: once for its own message and nine times as a neighbour of the chunks whose plants stand clear of its trunks over the border, about ten placings a chunk as a view loaded. They are now placed the first time they are asked for and held in the world's `sim::ground::Ground`, a bounded cache (`TREE_CACHE_CHUNKS`: four views with their rim, 1,764 chunks, about 0.5 MB on this valley, under 4 MB were every chunk the densest; the least recently used out first, D39) that the simulation thread and the server's threads share by handle, as they share the map: one placing for every reader, including the chunk-building pool to come. Each entry is keyed by the chunk and its **version**, 0 for every chunk until felling comes with the chunk's changes; a change bumps it (`Ground::changed`) and the chunk is placed again with the changes laid over, so the cache only ever holds what the seed gives (the plants' rule above). A hit and a miss give the same bytes; the fingerprints are unchanged. Measured (release, the 3 km valley, the machine shared, runs alternated): a chunk's own trees, as its message holds them, 43–72 µs → 1–2 µs; the trees of the nine chunks around it, as its plants read them, 357–599 µs → 4–7 µs (placed once, read nine times); in the one pair of runs the machine's load left comparable, a cold chunk of the login view 3.89 → 3.36 ms (−14 %) and of a teleport view 4.23 → 2.99 ms (−29 %), a login's 361 chunks 1.41 → 1.21 s, a teleport's 1.53 → 1.08 s, a warm chunk 165 → 97 µs (login) and 165 → 48 µs (teleport). On the quietest run before the change a cold chunk cost 2.36 ms, of which the neighbours' trees 0.36: the saving is the 13–15 % they were, not the 25 % the audit estimated from a slower pass; the plants are the rest, next (2b). Still nothing stored: the cache is derived state, empty on load.
  - **Woods along the waters, and dense and light regions, 11 November 2026** (the project lead's verdicts on the valley, in two rounds: a grass tile with plants in a brook's bed, a trunk standing in a brook, brooks hidden under closed corridors of trees, meadows along the rivers where a wild land would have its thickest woods; then, with a first riparian line tried, "the riverside band far too dense", and the wish that density itself vary as a landscape). The floodplain meadows (the pull falling within 40 tiles of a river) and the brook corridors (45 % of the tiles 1 to 2 off a brook, both sides) are replaced by a **riparian pull**. The reason: realism, water draws trees, and a primeval valley has its densest growth along its rivers; the player will clear those banks if they want them; and variety, so that a river is not the same all along. On screen: **the bank tile is always free** (nothing stands on a tile touching a river, a ford or, now, a brook, even by a corner: `trees::by_the_water`); the water's pull (480 thousandths at the thickest) is **strongest at the water's edge, held to 20 tiles from a river and fading smoothly to nothing at 60**, where the land is what it would have had on its own; a **brook draws the same shape, thinner** (300, held to 6 tiles, gone by 12). No line of trees of its own by the water, never a hedge: the bank has the woods of its region, and the water is no edge for the depth that thickens the forest (the wood by a river is as deep as the woods around it). The wood 2 to 5 tiles off a river (2 to 3 off a brook) is the **riverside habitat**, placed on the grids as any wood, the riverside kinds' (willow, alder) through the habitat alone. A **riparian noise** on cells of 256 tiles (`WOODS_RIPARIAN`) sets each stretch's thickness, from three tenths of the pull to all of it, so that one stretch is thick and the next thin; **below 140 thousandths a stretch is bare**: up to 900 thousandths of pull taken away instead as far as the wood would have gone, meadow to the water's edge: the lead wanted that possibility ("now and then it could be fields, even if very rare"). On the 3 km valley 39 per mille of the ground within 20 tiles of a river lie on a bare stretch. **Dense and light regions**: a **density noise** on cells of 300 tiles (`WOODS_DENSITY`, `Woods::stocking`) scales the chance every cell of the trees' grids holds a tree, and the meadows' groups', everywhere alike (massifs, small woods, copses, the woods by the water): all of it in a dense region, the heart as it was, 400 thousandths in a light one, the trees **about 1.6 times farther apart** (measured 1.5: 49 trees a thousand tiles of full forest against 23), the ground seen between the crowns, a light wood still a wood; easing from the one to the other between 100 and 350 thousandths of the noise, so most of the valley is dense or nearly (the mean nine tenths). A first try (160 thousandths, easing between 300 and 700, 97 868 trees) the lead found far too thin everywhere. The woods' density, the habitat, the forest's ground, the stands and the mother trees are the same in both: the multiplier sits on the placement chance, not on the density the kinds are drawn from, so the stand and mother mechanism is untouched. The massifs' level (`LEVEL`) goes from 432 to 505 so the forest stays at **553 per mille** of the 3 km valley's land (553 before); 153 782 trees (173 531 before: the light regions and the end of the old riverside line), among them 5 006 willows and 2 636 alders (6 755 and 4 198 before, when they lined every bank one a tile), the heart 4.3 trees a 100 m² over dense and light regions together (4.8 in a dense region, as before; 2.3 in a light one). **A brook's bed has no gaps**: a brook takes the tile each point of its path is in as well as the tiles within its half-width, and a land tile with brook on its left and right or above and below is closed after the brooks reach the water, so a brook is an 8-connected band at least a tile wide along its whole path; and every brook's stored current runs from its source to its mouth (pinned by a test; a brook seen running backwards in play is the client's drawing of it). The terrain, items and trees fingerprints are re-pinned on purpose.
  - **An adult's trunk blocks its tile, 12 November 2026** (lightness plan F2, D32): the trees are things standing on the ground, and a body no longer walks through them. The tiles a chunk's trunks stand in are a bitmap made with its trees and held with them (`ground::Trunks`); a dense heart is walked round trunk by trunk, and two trunks touching at a corner close the gap between them.
  - **The open ground inhabited, 15 November 2026** (the project lead's verdict on the vivarium, seed 14 zone 2: the hill and the slopes confirmed, but "huge plains have no plant and no tree"). A world cut from the atlas (D41) gives a whole 2 km cell one biome, and the valley's woods grew only where it was a forest: its grassland, 39 % of the zone, was bare, with no habitat at all, so neither a tree nor a plant. Now every dry tile outside a forest biome is a **meadow** (`Habitat::Meadow`), so the meadow's plants grow there and the valley's groups of trees come by the woods and the water as they do in the valley (so a forest's edge is never a wall: groups within 40 m of it, thinning out); and each open biome has **its own trees, as content** (`content/open_ground.ron`, read like `geology.ron`, D27): trees standing alone and groves a square kilometre, and a grove's size, drawn per cell of the meadows' grid (one group at most every 24 m) from the seed and the cell, thinned by the dense and light regions as the forest is; the kinds are the lone trees' of the region's stands. Placeholders: grassland 200 lone trees and 50 groves of 3 to 8 a square kilometre (about 5.8 trees a hectare on the zone, 168 groves and 336 lone trees on its 181 ha of grassland), wooded steppe 150 and 120 groves of 4 to 8, steppe 30 and 10, marsh 100 and 30; desert, tundra and alpine none. The forest itself is unchanged, and the valley (versions 1 and 2) has no biome and keeps its trees tile for tile. Bushes are the flora's, by habitat (`flora.ron`): nothing of theirs is set per biome yet.
  - **Keyed on the continent, 15 November 2026** (found by the safety net's sheets, D39: every 2 km window of the continent held the same woods, the same stands, clearings and groves, because the woods' noises and the round patches' cells were keyed on the window's own tiles, not on its place on the continent; the vivarium, one window, could not show it). Every layer drawn from the seed and a position is now keyed on the **world's position** (`Map::origin`, the window's corner on the continent; `gen::scatter::Frame`): the woods' noises, wetness and round patches, the trees' grids, stands and gaps, the plants, the rocks, the hills and the ragged borders of the atlas's windows. Two windows differ; the same ground drawn through two overlapping windows holds the same trees (a test on each); the valley, its own world at the origin, is byte-identical (terrain, woods, trees, plants and rocks hashed before and after). The vivarium's trees moved; its camps and fords did not. A round patch whose centre lies off a window still holds nothing on it, so a window's first tiles may differ from a neighbour's: a known seam, to settle when the continent is drawn in one piece.
  - Every number here is a dial of the generator, to be tuned by looking at maps; the mechanism is the decision.

### D38 — Development tools only behind `--dev`
- **Reason**: the valley is 3 km across and meant to grow far more; looking at a place far away (a river's mouth, a brook, a wood) should not take half an hour of walking. But any shortcut a client can ask for is a cheat the day other people play, so it must not exist in play at all (D05: the server decides).
- **Consequences** (8 November 2026): the server takes `--dev`. Only then does it send, after `world_state`, a `dev_overview` (the whole map, coarse: one letter a cell of at least 8 tiles, 512 cells across at most whatever the map's size), and accept `dev_teleport` (protocol v12), which the simulation plays as an intention (`Intention::Teleport`: the walkable tile nearest the target, within 64 tiles, at once). Without `--dev` there is no overview and a teleport is ignored. On the client, M opens the overview turned as the game shows the map, a click teleports; Ctrl+click on the ground does the same nearby. Further development tools go behind the same flag.

### D39 — Performance by design: measured, budgeted, never at the cost of what is seen
- **Reason**: the world grows richer (trees, then 39 species of flora, D37 and `content/ai-reserve/notes/10-flora.md`) and larger (3 km across, meant for ten times and more). Optimisations bolted on afterwards are costly and risky; laid down early, most cost little and change nothing on screen. The project lead's rule: lighter, but never at the cost of the look or the game. Examined on 9 November 2026: the wind in the trees is computed by the graphics card (a few operations a pixel, no redraw on the processor) and is kept; F4 lets the project lead measure it.
